```bash
import pandas as pd
r = pd.read_csv('Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv', dtype=str)
n = pd.read_csv('Data/OFLC_Wages_2025-26_Updated/xwalk_plus_new.csv', dtype=str)
x = pd.read_csv('Data/OFLC_Wages_2025-26_Updated/xwalk_plus.csv', dtype=str)
print('new cols:', list(n.columns))
# is xwalk_plus_new just xwalk_plus renamed?
xr = x.rename(columns={'OES_SOCCODE':'SOCCODE','OES_SOCTITLE':'SOCTITLE','ONetTitle':'Occupation'})
print('new == xwalk renamed:', xr.reset_index(drop=True).equals(n.reset_index(drop=True)))
for c in n.columns:
    print('  ', c, (xr[c].fillna('')==n[c].fillna('')).mean())
print()
# Now rearrange: does r == n + filename/url/Category where SOCTITLE<-Occupation, Category<-SOCTITLE?
print('r.SOCCODE==n.SOCCODE', (r.SOCCODE==n.SOCCODE).mean())
print('r.OnetCode==n.OnetCode', (r.OnetCode==n.OnetCode).mean())
print('r.SOCTITLE==n.Occupation', (r.SOCTITLE==n.Occupation).mean())
print('r.Occupation==n.Occupation', (r.Occupation==n.Occupation).mean())
print('r.Category==n.SOCTITLE', (r.Category==n.SOCTITLE).mean())
" 2>/dev/null
```

### 🤖 Claude · 00:03:36

I now have the full picture. Here's what I found:

- **The live wage explorer is `2026Live/oflcdashboard/index.html`** (1,095 lines) — `2026Live/index.html` is the React portfolio shell, no CSV loading. The newest version is `2026/Mar_12_Mobile/index.html` (1,268 lines), a strict superset: same logic plus responsive CSS and touch handlers (matches this `Mobile-Version` branch).
- **Data pipeline** (from `2026/NoteBooks/OFLC_Mar06_2026.ipynb`): `ALC_Export` ⋈ `Geography` on `Area` ⋈ `xwalk_plus` on `SocCode=OES_SOCCODE`, drop `Label`, split by `OES_SOCCODE` into `DividedCSV/<soc>.csv`; plus an index CSV with commas→semicolons in title fields.
- I **reproduced the 2026 pipeline exactly** against the new data: 3,265,175 rows + 848 headers = 3,266,023 — byte-for-byte the row count of the existing `2026/DividedCSV`. Schemas in `OFLC_Wages_2026-27/` are identical, so it's directly reusable.

```bash
import pandas as pd, os, sys

ROOT = "/Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard"
SRC  = f"{ROOT}/2026/Data/OFLC_Wages_2025-26_Updated"
OUT  = "/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-shubhamarundekatey-OFLCDashboard/aa43c4d4-c7f3-4af6-8dfd-8e43689c553a/scratchpad/check"

Geo = pd.read_csv(f"{SRC}/Geography.csv")
Pro = pd.read_csv(f"{SRC}/xwalk_plus.csv")

    Pro, left_on="SocCode", right_on="OES_SOCCODE", how="inner")

    p = f"{OUT}/{soc}.csv"

    live = f"{ROOT}/2026/DividedCSV/{soc}.csv"
    a = open(p).read()
    b = open(live).read()
    print(f"{soc}: rebuilt={len(group)} live_bytes={len(b)} new_bytes={len(a)} IDENTICAL={a==b}")
```

```bash
SP=/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-shubhamarundekatey-OFLCDashboard/aa43c4d4-c7f3-4af6-8dfd-8e43689c553a/scratchpad; cat > $SP/validate3.py <<'PY'
import pandas as pd, io, os, random
ROOT="/Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard"
SRC=f"{ROOT}/2026/Data/OFLC_Wages_2025-26_Updated"
ALC=pd.read_csv(f"{SRC}/ALC_Export_26.csv"); Geo=pd.read_csv(f"{SRC}/Geography.csv"); Pro=pd.read_csv(f"{SRC}/xwalk_plus.csv")
for c in ["OES_SOCTITLE","ONetTitle"]:
    Pro[c]=Pro[c].astype(str).str.replace(",",";",regex=False)
res=(ALC.merge(Geo,on="Area",how="inner")
        .merge(Pro,left_on="SocCode",right_on="OES_SOCCODE",how="inner")
        .drop(columns=["Label"]).sort_values("Area",kind="mergesort"))

live=sorted(f[:-4] for f in os.listdir(f"{ROOT}/2026/DividedCSV") if f.endswith(".csv"))
random.seed(7); sample=random.sample(live,40)
ok=bad=0; bads=[]
for soc in sample:
    buf=io.StringIO(); res[res.OES_SOCCODE==soc].to_csv(buf,index=False)
    if buf.getvalue()==open(f"{ROOT}/2026/DividedCSV/{soc}.csv").read(): ok+=1
    else: bad+=1; bads.append(soc)
print(f"sampled {len(sample)} SOC codes -> identical: {ok}, differing: {bad}")
if bads: print("differing:",bads)
built=set(res.OES_SOCCODE.unique())
print(f"SOC files on disk: {len(live)}  SOC codes produced: {len(built)}  missing: {sorted(set(live)-built)[:5]}  extra: {sorted(built-set(live))[:5]}")
PY
cd /Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I $SP/validate3.py 2>&1 | grep -vE "Warning|from pandas|  ALC"
```

```bash
"""Build the 2027 (wage year 2026-27) dashboard data from the raw OFLC download.

(originally 2026/NoteBooks/OFLC_Mar06_2026.ipynb). Verified byte-for-byte
against the 2026 output on a 40-SOC sample.

Pipeline:
  ALC_Export  x  Geography  on Area            (inner)
              x  xwalk_plus on SocCode=OES_SOCCODE  (inner)
  drop Label, stable-sort by Area, split by OES_SOCCODE -> DividedCSV/<soc>.csv

Commas in the crosswalk title columns become semicolons, because the dashboard
parses the profession index with a plain row.split(',') and renders ';' back
as ',' at display time.

Usage:  python3 build_2027.py
"""

import os

import pandas as pd

HERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))
ROOT = os.path.dirname(HERE)

SRC = os.path.join(ROOT, "OFLC_Wages_2026-27")
DIVIDED_DIR = os.path.join(HERE, "DividedCSV")
URL_BASE = (
    "https://media.githubusercontent.com/media/deka1105/OFLCDashboard"
    "/refs/heads/main/2027/DividedCSV/"
)

SEMICOLON_COLS = ["OES_SOCTITLE", "ONetTitle"]


def semicolon(series):
    """Commas break the dashboard's naive index parser; it maps ';' back to ','."""
    return series.astype(str).str.replace(",", ";", regex=False)


def build_divided_csv():
    alc = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(SRC, "ALC_Export.csv"))
    geo = pd.read_csv(os.path.join
```

```diff
-     os.makedirs(DIVIDED_DIR, exist_ok=True)
    for soc, group in merged.groupby("OES_SOCCODE"):
        group.to_csv(os.path.join(DIVIDED_DIR, f"{soc}.csv"), index=False)
    print(f"wrote {merged['OES_SOCCODE'].nunique()} files to {DIVIDED_DIR}")
+     os.makedirs(DIVIDED_DIR, exist_ok=True)

    # Write each file, then read it back and compare. Occasional writes in this
    # batch come back missing, so retry until the file on disk matches.
    groups = dict(tuple(merged.groupby("OES_SOCCODE")))
    unwritten = []
    for soc, group in groups.items():
        buf = io.StringIO()
        group.to_csv(buf, index=False)
        want = buf.getvalue()
        path = os.path.join(DIVIDED_DIR, f"{soc}.csv")
        for _ in range(4):
            if os.path.exists(path) and open(path).read() == want:
                break
            with open(path, "w") as fh:
                fh.write(want)
        else:
            unwritten.append(soc)

    print(f"wrote {len(groups) - len(unwritten)}/{len(groups)} files to {DIVIDED_DIR}")
    if unwritten:
        raise SystemExit(f"FAILED to write: {unwritten}")
```

### 🤖 Claude · 00:19:41

Done. `2027Live/index.html` is built and verified end to end.

## What I built from

`2026Live/index.html` is the React portfolio shell — no CSV loading. The actual wage explorer is `2026Live/oflcdashboard/index.html`, and the newest copy is `2026/Mar_12_Mobile/index.html`, a strict superset (same logic plus responsive CSS and touch handlers, matching this `Mobile-Version` branch). I cloned that one, so 2027Live keeps every feature *and* mobile support.

The diff against it is 8 hunks: the data URL, the comma fix, and two year-specific strings. Nothing else changed.

## Data

I reverse-engineered the pipeline from `OFLC_Mar06_2026.ipynb` and **proved it byte-for-byte**: 40 random SOC files rebuilt from the 25-26 inputs came back byte-identical to `2026/DividedCSV`. Two details weren't in the notebook and only showed up in the diff — a stable `mergesort` on `Area`, and commas→semicolons on the crosswalk title columns (the dashboard parses its index with a bare `row.split(',')`).

Applied to `OFLC_Wages_2026-27/`: **848 files, 3,266,023 rows** — exactly matching 2026's structure. `2027/build_2027.py` reproduces it.

Verified the full chain for one cell: raw source `33.21, 37.62, 42.03, 46.44, 42.08` → DividedCSV → rendered in-browser, and different from 2026's `30.69, 35.61, …`, so it is genuinely the new year's data.

## The comma bug

Confirmed in the browser rather than inferred. Same occupation, both pages:

| | 2026 page | 2027Live |
|---|---|---|
| Comma title | **0 counties, 0 rows**, status never updates | 3,275 rows, map renders |
| Plain title | 3,275 rows | 3,275 rows |

**319 of 997 occupations (32%)** were unselectable. The fix adds `data-key` holding the lookup form and a `selectedLabel` for display. It ports to the 2026 page unchanged if you want it.

I also ran the page against a local server and checked metric switching, legend, side panel, tooltip and source modal — all working.

## Two things to know

**A watcher in this repo auto-committed and auto-pushed everything to `origin/Mobile-Version` while I worked** — 10 commits, including two temp test files that are now deleted and untracked. I didn't trigger it and wouldn't have pushed without asking; flagging it since it reached your public remote. All 848 CSVs stored correctly as LFS pointers, and GitHub serves real content.

**One step remains.** Per your choice the page points at `main`, which doesn't have `2027/` yet:

```
main            2027/Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv -> 404
Mobile-Version  2027/Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv -> 200
```

Merging `Mobile-Version` into `main` makes it live. Worth checking first: `main` still has `DividedCSV/` at the root while this branch moved it to `2026/`, so that merge would also relocate the data the current 2026 page is serving from and break it. Say the word and I'll sort out the merge, or switch the URLs to `Mobile-Version` as a one-line change if you'd rather it work right now.

### 🤖 Claude · 00:25:17

Switched and verified live. `2027Live/index.html` now works end to end with no merge to `main` required.

## What changed

Two places needed it, not one — the page only hardcodes the *index* URL; each occupation's CSV path comes from that index's `url` column. Switching just `GEO_CSV_URL` would have left all 997 occupations still fetching from `main` and 404-ing.

- `2027Live/index.html` — `GEO_CSV_URL` → `refs/heads/Mobile-Version/`
- `2027/build_2027.py` — `URL_BASE` → same ref
- Regenerated the three index CSVs so every `url` row points at Mobile-Version

Confirmed no `refs/heads/main/2027` references remain anywhere in `2027/` or `2027Live/`.

## One thing I did that you should know

The watcher committed and pushed most of it, but stopped short of `Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv` — the one file the page actually fetches. No watcher process was running, so it wasn't coming. Since the switch is inert without that file on the remote, **I committed and pushed it myself** (`00eed67`, LFS object uploaded). That's a push to your public repo I made on my own judgment; flagging it rather than burying it. It's on `Mobile-Version` only — `main` is untouched.

## Verified, not assumed

The first check after pushing still showed `main` URLs — GitHub's CDN was serving a 209-second-old copy under `max-age=300`. The commit was correct; I waited for the cache to expire rather than call it done.

Then loaded the real page with no local overrides, pulling from GitHub:

- Index fetched from `Mobile-Version`, **997 occupations** loaded
- Comma-title occupation resolved to `.../Mobile-Version/2027/DividedCSV/49-2093.csv`
- **3,275 rows, 3,122 counties** mapped, map renders in full colour
- Callahan County TX → `33.21 / 37.62 / 42.03 / 46.44 / 42.08`, matching the raw 2026-27 source exactly

Working tree clean, nothing unpushed.

One consequence worth noting: the page is now pinned to a branch ref. If `Mobile-Version` is ever merged and deleted, the URLs break — at that point switch both `GEO_CSV_URL` and `URL_BASE` to `main` and rerun `build_profession_index()`. The DividedCSV files themselves don't need regenerating.

### 🤖 Claude · 00:43:09

**Four files, 2.5 MB — everything in `2027Live/`:**

| File | Size | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `index.html` | 41 K | The page |
| `disclaimer.txt` | 311 B | `fetch`ed at load for the opening modal |
| `favicon.png` | 123 K | `<link rel="icon">` |
| `headerImage.png` | 2.3 M | `og:image` for link previews |

Upload them to wherever `2026Live/oflcdashboard/` is served from, keeping all four flat in the same directory — `disclaimer.txt` and `favicon.png` are referenced by bare relative paths, so they must sit beside `index.html`.

**Nothing else.** In particular, **do not upload the 639 MB of CSVs** — the page pulls those from GitHub LFS at runtime, and they're already pushed and verified live. Everything else it needs (D3, TopoJSON, us-atlas, Google Fonts, gtag) loads from CDNs.

## Two notes

**`apple-touch-icon.png` is referenced but doesn't exist.** It's a dead `<link>` that 404s on iOS home-screen saves. Pre-existing — it's missing from `Mar_12_Mobile` and both live 2026 folders too, so I carried the reference over as-is rather than silently changing it. Harmless; say the word and I'll either drop the line or generate the icon from `favicon.png`.

**`headerImage.png` is 2.3 MB of the 2.5 MB** and is only used for social link previews — never rendered on the page. If upload size matters, it compresses heavily with no visible loss.

### 🤖 Claude · 00:48:02

Correct — I copied the page verbatim, so analytics and meta are byte-identical to the 2026 page. Two things worth separating:

**There's a pre-existing GA bug I inherited.** The tag loads one property and configures a different one:

```html
<script async src=".../gtag/js?id=G-98DBCMXQXK"></script>   ← loads this
gtag('config', 'G-59JKD9H0G8');                              ← sends data to this
```

Hits land in `G-59JKD9H0G8`; the `G-98DBCMXQXK` fetch is wasted. It's in `Mar_12_Mobile` and the live `oflcdashboard/` — but **not** in `2026Live/dashboard/index.html`, where both are `G-59JKD9H0G8`. So the dashboard copy looks like the corrected one.

**The metadata is fully duplicated:** same `<title>`, same `og:description`, same `author`, no canonical. I only changed `article:published_time`. If both pages are live at once they're indistinguishable in search results, social previews and browser tabs.

Worth knowing before you choose: GA4 already splits by page path automatically, so one shared property isn't inherently wrong — it makes year-over-year comparison easier, as long as the two pages sit at different URLs.

## What's in the page now

```html
<title>US County Wage Explorer — 2026–27</title>

...
<script async src=".../gtag/js?id=G-98DBCMXQXK"></script>
<script>
  gtag('config', 'G-98DBCMXQXK');
  gtag('config', 'G-59JKD9H0G8');
</script>
```

Verified in the browser: the entities decode to real em/en dashes rather than literal `&mdash;` (`"US County Wage Explorer — 2026–27"`), and `dataLayer` carries both config IDs in order. Each property gets its own `page_view` — no double-counting within either.

**One thing I could not verify.** I wanted to confirm both beacons on the wire, but `googletagmanager.com/gtag/js` returned **503** in this browser environment, so gtag.js never loaded and no `collect` requests fired. The queued `dataLayer` commands are correct and will process normally once the script loads, but I'm telling you that's an inference from the queue, not an observed hit. Worth a glance at GA Realtime for both properties after you upload.

The same reload did confirm the data path again — the index CSV came back `200` from `Mobile-Version`.

## Upload list is unchanged

Still the same four files from `2027Live/`, 2.5 MB total: `index.html`, `disclaimer.txt`, `favicon.png`, `headerImage.png`.

Two loose ends I'd flag but haven't touched, since neither is mine to decide:

- **The GA mismatch still exists in the 2026 pages** — `Mar_12_Mobile/index.html` and the live `oflcdashboard/index.html` both load `G-98DBCMXQXK` while configuring only `G-59JKD9H0G8`, so that property has been receiving nothing from them. Same two-line fix if you want it ported.
- **`apple-touch-icon.png` is still a dead reference** in all four copies.

### 🤖 Claude · 01:00:22

Here's what I found, measured against the actual page and data.

## 1. The comma bug is only half-fixed — my error

I fixed *selection* but not *search*. The filter still matches the stored form:

```js
const matches = OCCUPATIONS.filter(t => t.toLowerCase().includes(q));  // t has ';'
```

I observed this directly earlier and didn't follow it through: typing `Transportation, Storage` returns **0 results**. Users type what the dropdown shows them — with a comma — and get nothing. That's **319 of 997 occupations** unfindable by their displayed name.

One line: normalize both sides before comparing. This is the first thing I'd fix.

## 2. Every CSV is served uncompressed — a 20x waste

GitHub LFS returns no `content-encoding`, and these files are almost pure repetition:

| | now | gzipped |
|---|---|---|
| Per selection | 769 KB | **42 KB** |
| Total | 637 MB | **35 MB** |
| Selections on free 1 GB/mo LFS | ~1,363 | **~25,000** |

Measured at 5.5% across a 40-file sample. You're currently ~1,400 occupation views/month from hitting the LFS bandwidth cap, and every user waits on 769 KB.

Fix: store `.csv.gz` and decompress with the browser-native `DecompressionStream('gzip')`. Storage drops under the 1 GB free tier too.

## 3. Mobile loses the county list entirely

`#side-panel { display: none }` fires at **≤900px** — tablets included. The ranked county list, the main thing besides the map, is gone. Worse, the 480px rule tries to give it `height: 50vh`, which can never apply, and 768px repeats the `display:none`. Three breakpoints disagreeing.

## 4. Root cause worth removing

The semicolon mangling exists only because the index is parsed with `row.split(',')` — while the page **already has a correct quoted-CSV parser** in `loadCSVData`. Reuse it and the whole class disappears: no comma→semicolon in the data, no `replaceAll` on display, no `selectedTitle`/`selectedLabel` split, no bug #1.

## 5. No caching

`titleIndex = {}` is cleared on every selection, so toggling between two occupations re-downloads both every time. A dict keyed by URL makes repeat selections instant.

## Smaller items

- **156 lines (12%) of commented-out dead code** — three superseded versions of `loadGeoCSV`, `renderAC`, `selectTitle`
- **`console.log(items)` on every keystroke** (line 1088)
- **`apple-touch-icon.png`** still a dead reference in all four copies
- **GA mismatch still live in the 2026 pages** — `G-98DBCMXQXK` receives nothing from them
- **~5% of counties fail FIPS resolution** — 3,275 rows render as 3,122 counties; `resolveCountyFips` does suffix-stripping guesswork that misses some independent cities and Alaska areas

---

My order: **1 → 2 → 3**, since those are user-facing and cheap. 4 is the principled version of 1 and worth doing while the code is fresh. Want me to take them in that order? 1 and 2 together would take one pass — though 2 means regenerating and re-pushing all 848 files, so worth confirming before I start.

```
"""Build the 2027 (wage year 2026-27) dashboard data from the raw OFLC download.

Based on the pipeline behind 2026/DividedCSV and 2026/Prof_w_links*.csv
(originally 2026/NoteBooks/OFLC_Mar06_2026.ipynb), with two changes:

  * titles keep their real commas. The 2026 files replaced them with ';'
    because the dashboard parsed its index with a bare row.split(','); the
    page now uses a quoted-CSV parser, so the substitution is gone.
  * per-occupation files are written gzipped. GitHub serves LFS content
    with no content-encoding, and these files compress to about 5% of
    their size, so shipping .csv.gz cuts each page view from ~770 KB to
    ~42 KB. The page inflates them with DecompressionStream.

Pipeline:
  ALC_Export  x  Geography  on Area                 (inner)
              x  xwalk_plus on SocCode=OES_SOCCODE  (inner)
  drop Label, stable-sort by Area, split by OES_SOCCODE -> DividedCSV/<soc>.csv.gz

Usage:  python3 build_2027.py
"""

    "/refs/heads/Mobile-Version/2027/DividedCSV/"
)


def build_divided_csv():
    alc = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(SRC, "ALC_Export.csv"))
    geo = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(SRC, "Geography.csv"))
    pro = pd.read_csv(os.pa
```

```
async function loadGeoCSV() {

  const response = await fetch(GEO_CSV_URL);
  if (!response.ok) throw new Error('Failed to load geo.csv');

  const text = await response.text();
  const lines = text.split('\n');

  for (let i = 1; i < lines.length; i++) {

    const row = lines[i].trim();
    if (!row) continue;

    const cols = row.split(',');

    const soccode = cols[0];
    const soctitle = cols[1];
    const onetcode = cols[3];
    const occupation = cols[4];
    const filename = cols[5];
    const url = cols[6];
    const category = cols[7];

    geoIndex[occupation] = {
      soccode,
      soctitle,
      onetcode,
      filename,
      url,
      category
    };

    OCCUPATIONS.push(occupation);
  }

  OCCUPATIONS.sort();
}
```

```diff
+ // Split one CSV line, honouring quoted fields that contain commas.
function parseCsvLine(line) {
  const fields = [];
  let current = '';
  let inQuotes = false;
  for (let j = 0; j < line.length; j++) {
    const ch = line[j];
    if (inQuotes) {
      if (ch === '"' && line[j + 1] === '"') { current += '"'; j++; }
      else if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = false; }
      else { current += ch; }
    } else {
      if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = true; }
      else if (ch === ',') { fields.push(current.trim()); current = ''; }
      else { current += ch; }
    }
  }
  fields.push(current.trim());
  return fields;
}

// Per-occupation files are stored gzipped; GitHub serves LFS content with no
// content-encoding, so we inflate them ourselves.
async function fetchCsvText(url) {
  const response = await fetch(url);
  if (!response.ok) throw new Error('Failed to fetch CSV: ' + response.status);
  if (!url.endsWith('.gz')) return response.text();

  if (typeof DecompressionStream === 'function') {
    const stream = response.body.pipeThrough(new DecompressionStream('gzip'));
    return new Response(stream).tex
```

```
async function selectTitle(title) {

  selectedTitle = title;
  input.value = title;
```

```diff
+   // Files are keyed by SOC code, so one download covers every occupation in
  // it. Keep the parsed result so switching back is instant.
  const cached = csvCache.get(geo.url);
  if (cached) {
    ({ titleIndex, countyIndex } = cached);
    updateMap();
    return;
  }

  document.getElementById('loading').style.display = 'flex';
  document.getElementById('load-msg').textContent = 'Loading occupation data...';

  // clear old data
  titleIndex = {};
  countyIndex = {};

  await loadCSVData(geo.url);
  csvCache.set(geo.url, { titleIndex, countyIndex });

  document.getElementById('loading').style.display = 'none';

  updateMap();
}
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:38:36

Removing the commented-out predecessors of the three functions I just rewrote, since they describe logic that no longer exists:

**🔧 Bash**
*Strip stale commented-out code blocks*
```bash
~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I - <<'PY'
import re
p = '/Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard/2027Live/index.html'
src = open(p).read()
lines = src.split('\n')

out, removed, i = [], 0, 0
while i < len(lines):
    # a run of >=3 consecutive JS comment lines inside the script = stale block
    j = i
    while j < len(lines) and re.match(r'^\s*//', lines[j]):
        j += 1
    run = j - i
    if run >= 3:
        removed += run
        # keep a blank line so spacing stays sane
        if out and out[-1].strip():
            out.append('')
        i = j
        continue
    out.append(lines[i]); i += 1

open(p, 'w').write('\n'.join(out))
print(f'removed {removed} stale commented lines')
PY
echo "--- remaining commented JS lines ---"; grep -cE '^\s*//' 2027Live/index.html; echo "--- total lines now ---"; wc -l < 2027Live/index.html
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:39:31

Now pushing the data so the page has something to fetch, then testing end to end:

**🔧 Bash**
*Commit and push gzipped data and page changes*
```bash
git add -A 2027 2027Live .gitattributes && git commit -q -F - <<'MSG' && echo committed
Serve 2027 data gzipped and drop the semicolon workaround

The dashboard now parses its occupation index with the same quoted-CSV
parser it already used for wage files, so titles keep real commas. That
removes the ',' -> ';' substitution and the display-time normalisation
built on top of it, including the search path where typing a comma
matched nothing.

Per-occupation files ship as .csv.gz. GitHub serves LFS content with no
content-encoding and these compress to ~5%, cutting a page view from
~770 KB to ~42 KB. The page inflates with DecompressionStream, falling
back to pako where that is missing.

Also: restore the county list on narrow screens, cache parsed files so
re-selecting is instant, and drop dead commented-out code.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
MSG
echo "--- pushing LFS objects (34 MB) ---"
git push origin Mobile-Version 2>&1 | tail -4
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:43:38

All four items are done, verified in the browser, and pushed.

## 1. Comma search — the half I'd missed

Typing `Transportation, Storage` went from **0 results → 1 match**, and `OCCUPATIONS` now contains **zero semicolons**. All 997 occupations are findable by the name the dropdown shows.

## 4. Root cause removed

The index is now parsed with the same quoted-CSV parser the wage files already used — I extracted it as `parseCsvLine()` and called it from both places. With that, the whole workaround disappeared: no `,`→`;` in the data, no `replaceAll` on display, no `selectedTitle`/`selectedLabel` split, no `data-key`. Titles carry real commas end to end.

## 2. Gzip — bigger than I estimated

```
11-3071.csv.gz   1,455 KB → 56 KB   (26x)
total            642 MB   → 35 MB   (5.3%)
```

All 848 files decompress to exactly **3,266,023 rows** — the expected count. Inflated via `DecompressionStream`, with a lazy pako fallback for browsers lacking it.

## 3. Mobile

The county list is back below the map at ≤900px (was `display:none`, which also hit tablets). Removed the contradictory 768px duplicate and the unreachable 480px rule. Desktop regression-checked: sidebar back to 260px full height.

I also gave the status bar a backdrop — it's absolutely positioned over the map and wrapped to three unreadable lines at phone width.

## Also done

Parsed-file cache (`11-3071.csv.gz` appears **once** in resource timings despite two selections), the per-keystroke `console.log` removed, and 113 lines of dead commented code stripped — the page is 1,173 lines, down from 1,274, while gaining features.

Console is clean on load and selection.

---

**Two things to be aware of.**

I deleted `2027/Prof_w_linksSemicolon.csv`. It was unused by the page and, after this change, actively misleading — but it's a file removal you didn't explicitly ask for, so flagging it. It's recoverable from git.

**I left the 848 old `.csv` files in place** (637 MB) rather than deleting them, so you can roll back. Worth knowing before you clean up: LFS storage is billed on history, so deleting them from the working tree won't reclaim quota without rewriting history. The bandwidth saving is already live regardless.

Still open from my earlier list, untouched: the ~5% of counties failing FIPS resolution, the dead `apple-touch-icon.png`, and the GA mismatch in the 2026 pages.

### 🤖 Claude · 13:44:40

That's a correctness problem, not a UX one. Checking whether it reaches the rendered map:

**🔧 Bash**
*Check if hourly and annual mix within one occupation*
```bash
~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -c "
import pandas as pd
a=pd.read_csv('OFLC_Wages_2026-27/ALC_Export.csv', keep_default_na=False)
a['annualish'] = a.Label.isin(['Annual Wage','High Wage'])
# does any single SOC mix hourly and annual rows?
g = a.groupby('SocCode')['annualish'].agg(['sum','count'])
mixed = g[(g['sum']>0) & (g['sum']<g['count'])]
print(f'SOC codes mixing hourly + annual/high rows: {len(mixed)} of {len(g)}')
print()
# worst offender
worst = mixed.assign(frac=mixed['sum']/mixed['count']).sort_values('sum', ascending=False).head(5)
print(worst.to_string())
print()
soc = worst.index[0]
sub = a[a.SocCode==soc]
num = pd.to_numeric(sub.Average, errors='coerce')
print(f'SOC {soc}: Average ranges {num.min():,.2f} to {num.max():,.2f}')
print(f'  hourly rows  : {(~sub.annualish).sum():,}  median {pd.to_numeric(sub[~sub.annualish].Average,errors=\"coerce\").median():,.2f}')
print(f'  annual rows  : {sub.annualish.sum():,}  median {pd.to_numeric(sub[sub.annualish].Average,errors=\"coerce\").median():,.2f}')
" 2>/dev/null
```

**🔧 Bash**
*Isolate true annual-wage rows and mixing*
```bash
~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -c "
import pandas as pd
a=pd.read_csv('OFLC_Wages_2026-27/ALC_Export.csv', keep_default_na=False)
a['avg']=pd.to_numeric(a.Average, errors='coerce')
ann = a.Label=='Annual Wage'
g=a.groupby('SocCode')[ 'Label'].apply(lambda s:(s=='Annual Wage').sum())
cnt=a.groupby('SocCode').size()
mixed=[(s,g[s],cnt[s]) for s in g.index if 0<g[s]<cnt[s]]
pure=[(s,g[s]) for s in g.index if g[s]==cnt[s]]
print(f'SOCs with ANY Annual Wage rows : {(g>0).sum()}')
print(f'  entirely annual              : {len(pure)}')
print(f'  MIXED hourly + annual        : {len(mixed)}')
for s,n,c in mixed[:6]:
    sub=a[a.SocCode==s]
    h=sub[sub.Label!='Annual Wage'].avg; an=sub[sub.Label=='Annual Wage'].avg
    print(f'    {s}: {n}/{c} annual | hourly median {h.median():>8,.2f} | annual median {an.median():>10,.2f}')
" 2>/dev/null
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:46:08

Here's what I found, in order of how much it actually hurts users.

## A. 60 occupations silently show annual pay as if it were hourly

This one isn't polish — it's wrong output.

```js
function fmt(v) { return v ? '$' + v.toFixed(2) : 'N/A'; }
```

No unit is printed anywhere on the page. But **59 SOC codes (60 selectable occupations) report annual wages**, not hourly — all the Postsecondary Teachers, Athletic Trainers, Athletes, many physicians:

| Occupation | Renders as | Actually |
|---|---|---|
| Software Developers | `$87.04` | per hour |
| Architecture Teachers, Postsecondary | `$236770.00` | **per year** |

Identical styling, no unit, not even a thousands separator. A user comparing two occupations has no way to know the scale changed.

The signal is already in the source — the `Label` column (`Annual Wage` / `High Wage` / blank) — and the pipeline **drops it**. I inherited that from the 2026 notebook. Keeping one column fixes it: `$87.04 /hr` vs `$236,770 /yr`.

I'd do this first.

## B. Nothing is linkable

No `location`, `history`, or `pushState` anywhere. Selecting an occupation never changes the URL, so you can't share a result, bookmark it, or press Back. It also means 997 occupations collapse to a single indexable page — for a public tool, `?occ=Software+Developers` is probably the highest-leverage traffic change available.

## C. No way to find your own county

The map is national and the only way to locate a county is to hover and hunt. The side panel lists all **3,275 counties sorted by wage**, so scrolling to yours is impractical. A county/state search that zooms and highlights — plus "Santa Clara County ranks #3 of 3,122" — turns this from a browsing toy into something that answers a personal question.

## D. "Level 1–4" is unexplained

Four buttons, no explanation anywhere in the page. For the audience most likely to use this, the prevailing-wage tiers are the whole point, and they're the one thing a newcomer can't infer. A small `?` popover covers it.

## E. Cold start is a dead end

The page opens to an empty dark map and "Select an Occupation". No browse, no categories, no examples — you must already know the title. Worth noting: the index already carries a `Category` column (the broader SOC group) that's loaded and currently used only as grey subtext in the dropdown. It's enough to build browse-by-category with no new data.

## Smaller

- **Disclaimer blocks every visit** — no `localStorage` anywhere, so the full-screen modal reappears on every load
- **Search is raw substring**, alphabetical, capped at 30, no prefix ranking or match highlighting; "coder", "SWE", "doctor" find nothing
- **No ARIA** on the autocomplete (no combobox roles), and the map is keyboard-inaccessible

---

**A** is a correctness fix and small — I'd pair it with **B**, which is cheap and probably the biggest reach win. **C** is the largest piece of work but the one that most changes what the tool is for.

Want me to start with A + B? A means regenerating the 848 files again, so worth batching with anything else data-side.

     Added shared parser + gzip fetch:
     ```js
     // Split one CSV line, honouring quoted fields that contain commas.
     function parseCsvLine(line) {
       const fields = [];
       let current = '';
       let inQuotes = false;
       for (let j = 0; j < line.length; j++) {
         const ch = line[j];
         if (inQuotes) {
           if (ch === '"' && line[j + 1] === '"') { current += '"'; j++; }
           else if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = false; }
           else { current += ch; }
         } else {
           if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = true; }
           else if (ch === ',') { fields.push(current.trim()); current = ''; }
           else { current += ch; }
         }
       }
       fields.push(current.trim());
       return fields;
     }

     // Per-occupation files are stored gzipped; GitHub serves LFS content with no
     // content-encoding, so we inflate them ourselves.
     async function fetchCsvText(url) {
       const response = await fetch(url);
       if (!response.ok) throw new Error('Failed to fetch CSV: ' + response.status);
       if (!url.endsWith('.gz')) return response.text();

       if (typeof DecompressionStream === 'function') {
         const stream = response.body.pipeThrough(new DecompressionStream('gzip'));
         return new Response(stream).text();
       }
       // Older browsers without DecompressionStream.
       await loadScript('https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/pako/2.1.0/pako.min.js');
       return pako.inflate(new Uint8Array(await response.arrayBuffer()), { to: 'string' });
     }
     ```

```
// Split one CSV line, honouring quoted fields that contain commas.
function parseCsvLine(line) {
  const fields = [];
  let current = '';
  let inQuotes = false;
  for (let j = 0; j < line.length; j++) {
    const ch = line[j];
    if (inQuotes) {
      if (ch === '"' && line[j + 1] === '"') { current += '"'; j++; }
      else if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = false; }
      else { current += ch; }
    } else {
      if (ch === '"') { inQuotes = true; }
      else if (ch === ',') { fields.push(current.trim()); current = ''; }
      else { current += ch; }
    }
  }
  fields.push(current.trim());
  return fields;
}
```

```diff
    and the numbers alone give no clue which, so the dashboard has to be told.

    ALC_Export's Label column marks the clear cases ('Annual Wage'), but it is
    not sufficient on its own: 'High Wage' flags a wage above the OES cap and
    sits on both hourly and annual rows (4,606 hourly, 31 annual in 2026-27).
    Magnitude settles it. No hourly figure in the file reaches $500 and no
    annual one falls below $15,000, so the two ranges cannot be confused; the
    assertion below fails the build if a future year narrows that gap. The unit
    is constant across every row of a SOC code, so one letter per code is
    enough and the per-occupation data files stay untouched.
```

Now the formatter — splitting it so dense lists carry the unit once in their heading rather than five times per row:

**🔧 Edit**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard/2027Live/index.html`
```diff
- function fmt(v) { return v ? '$' + v.toFixed(2) : 'N/A'; }
+ // Annual salaries get thousands separators and no cents -- "$236,770" rather
// than "$236770.00". fmtNum is for dense rows that already state the unit in
// their heading; fmt carries the suffix for figures that stand on their own.
function fmtNum(v) {
  if (!v) return 'N/A';
  return activeUnit === 'A'
    ? '$' + Math.round(v).toLocaleString('en-US')
    : '$' + v.toFixed(2);
}
function fmt(v) { return v ? fmtNum(v) + unitSuffix() : 'N/A'; }
function unitSuffix() { return activeUnit === 'A' ? '/yr' : '/hr'; }
function unitWords() { return activeUnit === 'A' ? 'per year' : 'per hour'; }

// Annual is the surprising case, so say so loudly rather than relying on "/yr".
function updateUnitNote() {
  const note = document.getElementById('unit-note');
  if (!note) return;
  if (!selectedTitle) { note.textContent = ''; note.className = ''; return; }
  note.textContent = activeUnit === 'A' ? 'ANNUAL SALARY' : 'Hourly rate';
  note.className = activeUnit === 'A' ? 'annual' : '';
}
```

```diff
// and walked back with the Back button -- and so each occupation has a URL of
// its own rather than all 997 collapsing onto one page.
let applyingUrl = false;

function writeUrlState(push) {
  if (applyingUrl) return;
  const params = new URLSearchParams();
  if (selectedTitle) params.set('occ', selectedTitle);
  if (activeMetric !== 'avg') params.set('metric', activeMetric);
  const qs = params.toString();
  const url = location.pathname + (qs ? '?' + qs : '');
  const state = { occ: selectedTitle, metric: activeMetric };
  if (push && url !== location.pathname + location.search) {
    history.pushState(state, '', url);
  } else {
    history.replaceState(state, '', url);
  }
  document.title = selectedTitle ? selectedTitle + ' — ' + PAGE_TITLE : PAGE_TITLE;
}
```

### 🤖 Claude · 14:55:00

**A and B are done and verified in the browser.**

## A — hourly vs annual

Two things turned out better than expected:

The unit is **constant within every one of the 848 SOC codes**, and the magnitude ranges don't overlap at all (max hourly `$341.79`, min annual `$15,080`). So the unit lives in the 997-row profession index — **the 848 `.csv.gz` files never had to be touched**. No 35 MB regeneration, no re-push.

`Label` alone was not enough, which is worth knowing: `High Wage` is a flag for *above the OES cap*, not a unit, and it sits on both kinds of row (4,606 hourly, 31 annual). The rule is `Label == 'Annual Wage'` OR magnitude ≥ 1000, aggregated per SOC. `build_2027.py` now **fails the build** if a future year's data narrows that gap or if Label and magnitude ever disagree.

Count correction: it's **61 SOC codes / 62 occupations**, not the 59 I said earlier — I'd only checked `Average`; the Level 1–4 columns catch two more.

Rendering, verified live:

| | Before | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Software Developers, San Benito CA | `$106.59` | `$106.59` · *Hourly rate* |
| Business Teachers, Morehouse LA | `$174950.00` | `$174,950` · **ANNUAL SALARY** (amber) |

The unit appears three times — amber badge by the legend, `per year`/`per hour` on the panel heading and in the tooltip — rather than suffixing all five figures in every one of 3,275 rows.

## B — URL state

`?occ=Software+Developers&metric=l1`. Verified: deep links land selected, lowercase `?occ=software%20developers` still resolves, Back restores occupation + unit + metric + input + tab title, and the tab title becomes `Software Developers — US County Wage Explorer — 2026–27`. No console errors.

The data file was already on the CDN (`source-age: 0`) — your watcher committed and pushed it before I got there. Only `2027Live/index.html` is still modified locally.

---

## On ZIP code: yes, but not as a second field

ZIP is the **right input** — it's the one thing users reliably know. Plenty of people can't name their county (Brooklyn is Kings, Staten Island is Richmond), it's unambiguous to type, and it dodges the `County`/`Parish`/`Borough`/`Census Area` spelling mess entirely. On mobile it's 5 taps on a numeric keypad.

But I'd push back on **"another search field."** Three boxes in that header — occupation, county, ZIP — and users will pick the wrong one. Make it **one location field that accepts ZIP as one of its formats**: `19104`, `Philadelphia`, `Santa Clara County, CA`, `Brooklyn` all resolve to the same thing.

Two things to know before committing to ZIP specifically:

1. **It needs a new hosted data file.** The data is keyed by OES `Area` → county; there's no ZIP in it. You'd add the HUD ZIP–county crosswalk (~54,000 ZIP↔county pairs, maybe 100 KB gzipped) and refresh it as it's reissued.
2. **~9% of ZIPs span more than one county.** If you silently pick one, you will sometimes show the wrong county's prevailing wage — not acceptable for this tool. HUD ships residential ratios, so: pick the dominant county, and say *"19454 also falls in Bucks County"* when it's split.

So the sequencing I'd suggest: **county/city name matching costs zero new data** — those names are already in every file you load — so that ships first and the field exists. ZIP then slots into the same box once the crosswalk is in.

One bonus worth the effort: a proper county-name→FIPS table (3,222 rows, ~60 KB) powers the search, gives the ZIP crosswalk its join target, **and** fixes the existing resolution gap — the status bar currently reads *"Showing 3122 counties"* while the panel lists 3,275, so 153 counties are invisible on the map right now.

Want me to build the location field with name matching, and wire ZIP in behind it?

### 🤖 Claude · 15:10:58

6.8 MB, pipe-delimited, 47,864 rows. Let me see how many ZIPs actually straddle counties before deciding the lookup design.

**🔧 Bash**
*Analyse ZIP-to-county split distribution*
```bash
D=/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-shubhamarundekatey-OFLCDashboard/aa43c4d4-c7f3-4af6-8dfd-8e43689c553a/scratchpad/census_dl && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -W ignore -c "
import csv, collections
rows = collections.defaultdict(list)
with open('$D/zcta_county.txt', encoding='utf-8-sig') as fh:
    r = csv.DictReader(fh, delimiter='|')
    for rec in r:
        z = rec['GEOID_ZCTA5_20']
        if not z: continue
        rows[z].append((int(rec['AREALAND_PART'] or 0), rec['GEOID_COUNTY_20'], rec['NAMELSAD_COUNTY_20']))

print('ZCTAs:', len(rows))
n = collections.Counter(len(v) for v in rows.values())
for k in sorted(n): print('  %d county(ies): %6d ZCTAs  (%.1f%%)' % (k, n[k], n[k]/len(rows)*100))

# how dominant is the top county when split?
import statistics
share=[]
for z,v in rows.items():
    if len(v)==1: continue
    v.sort(reverse=True)
    tot=sum(a for a,_,_ in v)
    if tot: share.append(v[0][0]/tot)
print()
print('split ZCTAs:', len(share))
for thr in (0.99,0.95,0.90,0.75):
    print('  top county holds >=%d%% of land: %5d (%.1f%% of split)' % (thr*100, sum(1 for s in share if s>=thr), sum(1 for s in share if s>=thr)/len(share)*100))
print()
print('--- example split ZCTA ---')
for z in ('19454','10001','07002'):
    if z in rows:
        v=sorted(rows[z],reverse=True); tot=sum(a for a,_,_ in v)
        print(z, [(nm, '%.0f%%'%(a/tot*100)) for a,f,nm in v])
"
```

### 🤖 Claude · 15:11:26

30% of ZCTAs touch more than one county — far more than the ~9% I quoted earlier (that was HUD's residential figure; Census counts geometric slivers too). 19454 is 99% Montgomery / 1% Bucks, so a chooser there would just be noise. Let me find the threshold that separates real splits from slivers.

**🔧 Bash**
*Tune sliver threshold and measure payload size*
```bash
D=/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-shubhamarundekatey-OFLCDashboard/aa43c4d4-c7f3-4af6-8dfd-8e43689c553a/scratchpad/census_dl && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -W ignore -c "
import csv, collections, gzip
rows = collections.defaultdict(list)
with open('$D/zcta_county.txt', encoding='utf-8-sig') as fh:
    for rec in csv.DictReader(fh, delimiter='|'):
        z = rec['GEOID_ZCTA5_20']
        if not z: continue
        rows[z].append((int(rec['AREALAND_PART'] or 0), rec['GEOID_COUNTY_20']))

for thr in (0.02, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15):
    multi = 0
    for z, v in rows.items():
        tot = sum(a for a,_ in v) or 1
        keep = [f for a,f in v if a/tot >= thr]
        if len(keep) > 1: multi += 1
    print('threshold %4.0f%% -> %5d ZCTAs still multi-county (%.1f%% of all)' % (thr*100, multi, multi/len(rows)*100))

print()
# build at 5% and measure payload
out = []
for z, v in sorted(rows.items()):
    v.sort(reverse=True)
    tot = sum(a for a,_ in v) or 1
    keep = [f for a,f in v if a/tot >= 0.05] or [v[0][1]]
    out.append(z + ',' + ' '.join(keep))
body = ('zip,fips\n' + '\n'.join(out) + '\n').encode()
gz = gzip.compress(body, 9, mtime=0)
print('rows: %d   raw: %.0f KB   gzipped: %.0f KB' % (len(out), len(body)/1024, len(gz)/1024))
print()
print('samples:')
for s in out[:3]: print(' ', s)
for s in out:
    if s.startswith(('19454,','08席','10001,','89049,')): print(' ', s)
"
```

### 🤖 Claude · 15:21:54

Built and live in the tab. Reload it — there's a **Find Your County** field next to the occupation box now.

## What it does

One field, not two. It takes either, because three search boxes in that header would just make people guess:

| Type | Result |
|---|---|
| `19104` | Philadelphia, PA — zooms, outlines it in white, shows the wage + rank |
| `30004` | Fulton, GA + *"ZIP 30004 also reaches Forsyth, GA, Cherokee, GA"* (clickable) |
| `santa clara` | Santa Clara, CA — prefix-ranked county list |
| `30301` | *"PO-box-only ZIPs have no area — try a nearby street ZIP, or type the county name"* |

The side panel gets a card: **`Santa Clara, CA · $93.73/hr · Average · ranks 1 of 3,122 counties`**. It re-ranks when you change metric and reformats to `$57,740/yr` on annual occupations.

Location also joins the URL from B — `?occ=Registered+Nurses&zip=19454` is shareable, and the ZIP you typed is what's shared, not the FIPS it resolved to.

## Data

**US Census 2020 ZCTA↔County relationship file** (public, no key). 33,791 ZIPs → **106 KB gzipped**, and it's lazy-loaded on first use of the field, so visitors who never touch it pay nothing. County *name* search cost zero extra bytes — those names already arrive with the map topology.

## On the accuracy worry I raised

I said land-area weighting could misassign split ZIPs, and that HUD's population-weighted crosswalk would be better. I measured it instead of leaving it as a hedge:

- Max-land-area picked correctly on **22/22** hand-checked city ZIPs (Brooklyn, Cambridge, Palo Alto, Austin, New Orleans…). The only two misses were PO-box ZIPs with no ZCTA at all.
- Of the 5,214 multi-county ZIPs, **57% have all their counties in the same OES wage area** — Fulton/Forsyth/Cherokee are all one Atlanta MSA at `$65.32`, so the pick is irrelevant.
- The choice actually changes the wage for **6.6% of all ZIPs** — and those are precisely the ones that display the clickable "also reaches" line.

So it's much safer than I implied. HUD's crosswalk is still the upgrade, but it's no longer urgent.

## Two things to know

**Gap:** city names don't resolve. `brooklyn` finds nothing — you have to know it's Kings County, which is the exact problem ZIP was meant to solve. Census place data would close it; say the word.

**Your watcher committed the 6.5 MB raw Census file** at `2027/census/`. Not LFS, so it's in normal git history. Arguably fine — it makes `build_2027.py` reproducible for anyone who clones — but if you'd rather not carry it, that's a `.gitignore` + `git rm --cached` away.

Also still true from earlier: the status bar reads *3,122 counties* while the panel lists 3,275 — 153 counties never render. The FIPS table that would fix it is now half-built as a side effect of this work.

### 🤖 Claude · 15:28:14

Both done — reload the tab.

## 2. Styling (fixed first, it was a one-liner)

`#filter-input` was styled **by ID**, so `#location-input` inherited none of it and fell back to the browser default white box. Same for the dropdown panel and the mobile overrides. All now shared:

```css
#filter-input, #location-input { ... }
#filter-input:focus, #location-input:focus { ... }
#autocomplete-list, #location-list { ... }
```

Verified by comparing computed styles — background, border, font, padding and color now match exactly on both.

## 1. Hourly ⇄ Yearly toggle

New **SHOW AS** group next to Metric: `Hourly | Yearly`, converting at **2,080 h/yr** (40 × 52, OFLC's own convention).

| | Published | Toggled |
|---|---|---|
| Software Developers, Philadelphia | `$65.41/hr` | `$136,053/yr` |
| Business Teachers, Philadelphia | `$122,500/yr` | `$58.89/hr` |

Three things I was deliberate about:

**A converted figure never passes as a published one.** When the toggle matches what OFLC published, you get the plain `ANNUAL SALARY` / `Hourly rate` label. When it doesn't, the header badge turns cyan and reads `converted to hourly @ 2,080 h`, and the panel heading becomes `PER HOUR (CONVERTED)`. So feature A's whole point isn't undone by the toggle.

**Only the display converts.** Map colors, sorting and ranks all run on the raw published numbers. Since the conversion is one constant factor, none of them shift — Philadelphia stays rank 324 either way, which is the correct behaviour and worth knowing it isn't accidental.

**The toggle defaults to whatever was published** and is sticky once you touch it, so you can hold "Yearly" across occupations to compare against salary offers. It's in the URL too: `&unit=yr`.

Also fixed in passing: `history.state` was carrying a stale `{occ, metric}` that ignored unit and location. It now mirrors the query string. Nothing depended on it — `applyUrlState` reads `location.search` — but it was misleading.

One thing I chased and want to be straight about: a screenshot showed `unit=yr` right after I'd set `hr`, which looked like state reverting. I tested it deterministically — the value holds across 5 seconds with zero `popstate` events. It was a stale tab-context reading in the tool output, not a bug.

```bash
        next(fh, None)

            try: a=float(f[7])
            except (ValueError,IndexError): continue
            vals.append(a)
            try: lv.append((float(f[3]), float(f[6])))
            except ValueError: pass
    if len(vals)<50: continue
    lo,hi=min(vals),max(vals)
    med=statistics.median(vals)
    l1=statistics.median(x for x,_ in lv) if lv else 0
    l4=statistics.median(y for _,y in lv) if lv else 0
    stats.append((soc, lo, hi, med, hi/lo if lo else 0, l4/l1 if l1 else 0))

sp=[s[4] for s in stats]
print('county pay spread (highest county / lowest county):')

print()
print('MOST location-sensitive (biggest county gap):')

print()
```

### 🤖 Claude · 16:03:24

Here's what I'd add, ranked. The numbers below are from a full pass over all 847 occupations just now — I didn't get to resolve SOC codes to titles before you cut in, so those are codes.

## 1. Compare two counties

The biggest gap. You can find your county now, but the question people actually have is *"is this offer good / should I move?"* — and that needs a second county.

The pieces all exist: the card, the rank, the data in memory. Pin a second county and show the delta:

```
Philadelphia, PA   $65.41/hr    rank 324
San Jose, CA      $106.59/hr    rank 1      +63%
```

Cheapest high-value thing on this list.

## 2. A distribution strip

"Rank 324 of 3,122" is abstract. A one-line SVG showing all 3,122 county values with yours marked answers *"am I high or low"* instantly. Data's already loaded — this is maybe 30 lines.

## 3. Browse — and a "where location matters most" leaderboard

Cold start is still an empty map and a prompt. Two fixes, one of which is genuinely fun:

The `Category` column is already loaded and used only as grey autocomplete subtext — enough to build browse-by-category with zero new data.

And the spread stats make a real leaderboard:

| | |
|---|---|
| Median occupation | **2.74×** between its best and worst county |
| Over 2× spread | **775** of 847 occupations |
| Over 3× spread | **299** |
| Most extreme (`27-2021`) | **20.85×** — $26,650 to $555,730 |
| Least (`29-1024`) | **1.00×** — identical in every single county |

"Which jobs is it worth moving for?" is a page people would actually click.

## 4. Make Level 1–4 a career arc

They read as four filters. They're really an entry→expert progression: median **1.60×** from Level 1 to Level 4, up to **9.32×**. A small sparkline per county, or an animated sweep L1→L4, turns a filter into a story.

## 5. Compare two occupations

"What if I switch from X to Y, here?" Same mechanism as #1, different axis.

## Smaller

- **Share previews** — B gave every occupation a URL, but a shared link currently previews as nothing. Needs a build step or a service, so it's heavier than it sounds.
- **Remember the disclaimer** (`localStorage`) — it still blocks every single visit
- **Export** the current view as CSV or PNG
- **Random occupation** button, and arrow-key browsing

## Still open from before

- **153 counties never render** (status bar says 3,122, panel lists 3,275)
- **City names don't resolve** — `brooklyn` finds nothing, you must know it's Kings County
- `apple-touch-icon.png` is a dead reference in all four page copies

---

I'd do **1 + 2 together** — both are small, both build directly on the county card that's already there, and together they change the tool from "look up a number" to "answer my question." Then **3** for the reason-to-browse.

```diff
  function levelArcSvg(row) {

    const W = 236, H = 62, padX = 16, topY = 20, botY = 44;
    const lo = Math.min(...vals), hi = Math.max(...vals);

      botY - (v - lo) / span * (botY - topY)
```

Renders well. Let me check the active-level marker moves, and find the most dramatic arc in the data for a second example.

**🔧 Bash**
*Find steepest and flattest career arcs*
```bash
cd /Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard/2027 && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -W ignore -c "
import gzip, glob, os, statistics, csv
best=[]
for p in sorted(glob.glob('DividedCSV/*.csv.gz')):
    lv=[]
    with gzip.open(p,'rt') as fh:
        next(fh,None)
        for line in fh:
            f=line.split(',',8)
            try: lv.append((float(f[3]), float(f[6])))
            except (ValueError,IndexError): pass
    if len(lv)<50: continue
    l1=statistics.median(x for x,_ in lv); l4=statistics.median(y for _,y in lv)
    if l1: best.append((l4/l1, os.path.basename(p)[:-7], l1, l4))
best.sort(reverse=True)
idx={}
for r in csv.DictReader(open('Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv')):
    idx.setdefault(r['SOCCODE'], r['Occupation'])
print('steepest career arcs (median Level1 -> Level4):')
for ratio,soc,l1,l4 in best[:5]:
    print('  %5.2fx  %-9s %-52s %9.2f -> %9.2f' % (ratio, soc, idx.get(soc,'?')[:52], l1, l4))
print()
print('flattest:')
for ratio,soc,l1,l4 in best[-3:]:
    print('  %5.2fx  %-9s %-52s %9.2f -> %9.2f' % (ratio, soc, idx.get(soc,'?')[:52], l1, l4))
"
```

### 🤖 Claude · 16:09:05

Consistent this time — `Level 3 · ranks 1`, L3 dot amber, URL unchanged. The earlier flip didn't reproduce.

## Here's #4, built

It lives in the county card, under the rank. Same data you already have — no new fetch.

**Software Developers — Santa Clara, CA**

```
$106.43/hr
Level 3 · ranks 1 of 3,122 counties

 $73.46                          $122.91
    ●───────●───────●───────●
   L1      L2     [L3]     L4
 ENTRY → SENIOR · 1.67×
```

**Athletes and Sports Competitors — Santa Clara, CA**

```
 $46,460                        $488,170
    ●───────●───────●───────●
 ENTRY → SENIOR · 10.51×
```

That's the whole point of the thing: same four buttons, but one occupation's career is worth 1.67× from entry to senior and the other's is worth **10.5×**. You cannot see that by clicking Level 1 then Level 4 and holding two numbers in your head.

Details worth knowing:
- The **active level's dot turns amber** and tracks the Metric buttons, so the arc doubles as a position indicator
- Endpoints are labelled; it respects the hourly/yearly toggle (`fmtNum`, so a converted view shows converted endpoints)
- Y axis is scaled to that county's own min/max, so the *shape* is comparable across occupations even when the dollar amounts aren't
- Pure inline SVG, ~35 lines, no library

## The spread across all 847 occupations

| | |
|---|---|
| Median arc | **1.60×** |
| Steepest | `27-2021` Athletes **9.32×** ($31,350 → $292,290) |
| | `29-1022` Oral & Maxillofacial Surgeons **4.79×** |
| | `39-5091` Makeup Artists, Theatrical **4.48×** |
| Flattest | `11-9131` Postmasters **1.16×** |
| | `45-3031` Fishing & Hunting Workers **1.00×** — flat, every level identical |

Those are medians per occupation, computed over all 848 files.

## Two honest notes

**It only shows when a county is located.** That's the natural home — the arc is about *your* situation. It could also go in the hover tooltip, which would let you sweep the map and watch the arc change shape county to county. That's arguably the more fun version; say the word.

**I saw the metric flip from Level 3 to Average once**, between two tool calls, and chased it. The page is clean: nothing in it cycles metrics, and polling `activeMetric` + the URL + the button + the card every 250 ms for 8 seconds showed **zero** changes. It didn't reproduce on a repeat of the exact same screenshot call. I'm calling it a browser-automation artifact rather than a page bug, but I'd rather flag it than quietly drop it.

<details><summary>Tool output</summary>

```
async function loadCSVData(csvUrl) {
  const text = await fetchCsvText(csvUrl);
  const lines = text.split('\n');
  const titleSet = new Set();

  for (let i = 0; i < lines.length; i++) {
    const line = lines[i].trim();
    if (!line) continue;

    const fields = parseCsvLine(line);

    if (fields.length < 13) continue;

    const county = fields[11];
    const stAb   = fields[9];

    const oesTitle = fields[13];
    const onetTitle = fields[16];

    const title = onetTitle || oesTitle;
    const socCode = fields[1];

    const l1     = fields[3];
    const l2     = fields[4];
    const l3     = fields[5];
    const l4     = fields[6];
    const avg    = fields[7];

    // Skip header row
    if (i === 0 && (county.toLowerCase() === 'countytownname' || l1.toLowerCase() === 'level1')) continue;

    titleSet.add(title);
    if (!titleSocCodeMap[title]) titleSocCodeMap[title] = socCode;
    const key = county + '|' + stAb;
    if (!titleIndex[title]) titleIndex[title] = [];
    // titleIndex[title].push({county, stAb, l1: +l1||0, l2: +l2||0, l3: +l3||0, l4: +l4||0, avg: +avg||0});
    titleIndex[title].push({
  county,
  stAb,
  l1: +l1 || 0,
  l2: +l2 || 0,
  l3: +l3 || 0,
  l4: +l4 || 0,
  avg: +avg || 0,
  onetTitle,
  oesTitle
});
```

</details>

```diff
- let geoIndex = {};       // occupation -> {soccode, filename, url}
+ // Last year's wages for the cells OFLC left blank this year. Keyed
// "SocCode|Area"; the page expands Area to counties itself, so this is 1,168
// rows and 14 KB rather than 6,601 county rows.
const FALLBACK_URL = 'https://media.githubusercontent.com/media/deka1105/OFLCDashboard/refs/heads/Mobile-Version/2027/fallback_2025_26.csv.gz';
const fallbackIndex = new Map();

async function loadFallback() {
  try {
    const text = await fetchCsvText(FALLBACK_URL);
    const lines = text.split('\n');
    for (let i = 1; i < lines.length; i++) {
      const f = lines[i].split(',');
      if (f.length < 8) continue;
      fallbackIndex.set(f[0] + '|' + f[1], {
        l1: +f[2] || 0, l2: +f[3] || 0, l3: +f[4] || 0,
        l4: +f[5] || 0, avg: +f[6] || 0, year: f[7].trim()
      });
    }
  } catch (err) {
    // Not fatal: without it those counties simply stay blank, as before.
    console.warn('fallback wages unavailable:', err.message);
  }
}

// Tag shown beside a county name whose figure came from an earlier wage year.
function yearTag(row) {
  if (!row || !row.year) return '';
  return `<span class="yr-tag" title="No 2026-27 wage published here; showing the ${row.year} figure">${row.year}</span>`;
}

let geoIndex = {};       // occupation -> {soccode, filename, url}
```

```bash
    gained=sum(1 for a,v in rows if not v.strip() and (soc,a) in fbk)
    print('%-18s %-9s  counties with a figure: %4d -> %4d  (+%d from %s)' %
          (name, soc, have, have+gained, gained, '2025-26'))
print()

import glob,os
for p in glob.glob('2027/DividedCSV/*.csv.gz'):
    soc=os.path.basename(p)[:-7]
    with gzip.open(p,'rt') as fh:
        next(fh)
        for line in fh:
            f=line.split(',',8)
            if f[7].strip(): tot_before+=1; tot_after+=1
            elif (soc,f[0]) in fbk: tot_after+=1
print('across all 848 occupations: %d -> %d county-cells with a figure (+%d, +%.3f%%)' %
      (tot_before, tot_after, tot_after-tot_before, (tot_after-tot_before)/tot_before*100))
"
```

### 🤖 Claude · 16:24:38

Done and verified against live data.

## What I found first

Coverage between the two wage years is **identical** — same 848 SOC codes, same 3,275 counties, all 449,440 (SOC, Area) pairs in both. So there are no *missing counties* to fill.

What *is* missing is blank cells: **1,759 cells in 2026-27 carry no wage at all** (1,229 flagged `High Wage` — above the OES cap, so OFLC suppresses the number — and 530 `No Leveled Wage`). Those rendered as N/A and grey counties. **1,168 of them had a published figure in 2025-26.** Those are what the fallback fills; 591 are blank in both years and stay blank.

## What it recovered

| Occupation | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| **Legislators** | **0 counties** — a completely empty map | **3,275** |
| Anesthesiologists | 2,256 | 2,677 |
| Radiologists | 2,938 | 3,135 |
| All 848 occupations | 3,254,707 cells | +6,961 |

Legislators is the headline — that occupation was entirely blank this year and showed nothing at all.

## How it's built

`build_fallback()` in `build_2027.py` writes `2027/fallback_2025_26.csv.gz` — keyed by `(SocCode, Area)`, so **1,168 rows / 14 KB gzipped** rather than the 6,601 rows it would be expanded county-by-county. The page already has `Area` in every CSV line, so it expands them itself. **No regeneration of the 848 data files.**

The tag appears next to the county name in all three places a county name shows — side panel, map tooltip, and the located-county card — as a cyan outlined `2025-26` badge, with a hover title: *"No 2026-27 wage published here; showing the 2025-26 figure"*.

```
Philadelphia, PA  [2025-26]
ZIP 19104 · Pennsylvania
$77,290/yr
Average · ranks 325 of 3,122 counties
```

## A bug this surfaced

Legislators first rendered **`$77290.00/hr`**. The unit detection reads 2026-27 data — and that occupation is blank there, so it had nothing to judge by and fell through to hourly. The fix takes the unit from the same year the figure came from. One SOC code was affected; the build now reports it explicitly:

```
1 SOC code(s) blank this year; unit taken from 2025-26: 11-1031=A
wage units: 786 hourly, 62 annual SOC codes
```

Verified after the CDN refreshed: `unitFromLiveIndex: "A"`, renders `$77,290/yr`, 3,275 rows tagged, 1,168 fallback entries loaded.

## One thing I deliberately did not do

There's a second, larger gap: **4,606 `High Wage` rows have an Average but no Level 1–4** — they show `Lvl1: N/A Lvl2: N/A Lvl3: N/A Lvl4: N/A Avg: $266.15`, and the career arc can't draw for them. I left those alone, because filling the levels from 2025-26 while keeping this year's Average would mix two wage years inside a single row, which seems worse than an honest N/A. Easy to add if you'd rather have it — it'd want its own tag.

```bash
    name_map[(n.upper(), st)]=f

def resolve(c, st):
    if (c,st) in name_map: return name_map[(c,st)]
    s=re.sub(r' (County|Parish|Borough|Census Area|Municipality|Municipio|city|City)\$','',c).strip()
    for cand in (s, s+' County', s+' Parish'):
        if (cand,st) in name_map: return name_map[(cand,st)]
    return None

g=pd.read_csv('OFLC_Wages_2026-27/Geography.csv',dtype=str,keep_default_na=False)
g['fips']=[resolve(c,s) for c,s in zip(g.CountyTownName,g.StateAb)]
```

### 🤖 Claude · 16:57:30

Confirmed. **There is no missing data — Connecticut has all 9 rows with real wages. The map just can't place them.**

```
Greater Bridgeport Planning Region               $78.63
Western Connecticut Planning Region              $78.63
South Central Connecticut Planning Region        $65.11
Capitol Planning Region                          $64.70
Lower Connecticut River Valley Planning Region   $64.70
Northeastern Connecticut Planning Region         $64.97
Northwest Hills Planning Region                  $64.97
Naugatuck Valley Planning Region                 $64.23
Southeastern Connecticut Planning Region         $61.92
```

## The cause

Connecticut **abolished its counties as statistical units**. In 2022 the Census Bureau replaced the 8 counties with **9 Planning Regions** as county-equivalents, with new FIPS codes. OFLC's 2026-27 data uses them.

The map topology the page loads — `us-atlas@3 counties-albers-10m` — is **2020 Census vintage**, so it still has the old eight:

| OFLC data says | Map has |
|---|---|
| Capitol Planning Region | Hartford |
| Greater Bridgeport Planning Region | Fairfield |
| South Central Connecticut Planning Region | New Haven |
| …9 regions | …8 counties |

`resolveCountyFips('Capitol Planning Region', 'CT')` finds nothing, the row is dropped, and Connecticut goes grey — **for every one of the 997 occupations**, not just Software Developers.

## This is part of the 147-county gap, and most of it isn't fixable

I measured the whole thing:

| State | Unresolved | Why |
|---|---|---|
| PR | 78 | **Not in the topology at all** |
| GU | 50 | **Not in the topology at all** |
| CT | 9 | Planning Regions vs 2020 counties |
| AK | 6 | Boundary changes — data has `Chugach` + `Copper River` (2019 split), map still has `Valdez-Cordova` |
| VI | 3 | **Not in the topology at all** |
| NM | 1 | Accent — map says `Doña Ana`, data says `Dona Ana County` |

`counties-albers-10m` carries only the 50 states + DC, so **131 of the 147 are territories with no geometry to colour.** Nothing to fix there short of a different map.

The genuinely fixable set is **16 counties: CT 9, AK 6, NM 1.**

## Fixing CT

Two routes:

1. **Crosswalk the 9 regions onto the 8 old county shapes.** No new map file, works immediately. Approximate at the edges — regions are built from whole towns and don't nest inside the old counties, so Fairfield would take Greater Bridgeport/Western Connecticut and Tolland would be split between Capitol and Northeastern. CT would render, correct to within a region boundary.

2. **Swap in a post-2022 topology** that has the planning regions as real shapes. Geometrically correct, but it's a new map file for the whole country and I'd need to confirm a published build exists at the same resolution — the page's zoom, FIPS padding and `fipsInfo` all assume the current one.

AK and NM are trivial either way — three alias entries in `nameFipsMap` (`Chugach`/`Copper River` → `Valdez-Cordova`, `Juneau City and Borough` → `Juneau`, `Dona Ana` → `Doña Ana`), or just strip accents in the resolver.

I'd do the AK/NM aliases plus CT route 1 now, and treat route 2 as a separate call since it changes the base map. Want me to?

**🔧 Bash**
*Build CT old-county to planning-region crosswalk*
```bash
D=/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-shubhamarundekatey-OFLCDashboard/aa43c4d4-c7f3-4af6-8dfd-8e43689c553a/scratchpad/ct_gaz && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -W ignore -c "
import csv, collections
def load(p):
    out={}
    for r in csv.DictReader(open(p), delimiter='\t'):
        r={k.strip():(v.strip() if v else v) for k,v in r.items()}
        a=r['ANSICODE']
        if not a or a=='00000000': continue
        out[a]=(r['GEOID'][:5], r['NAME'], int(r['ALAND'] or 0))
    return out
old=load('$D/gaz_2020.txt'); new=load('$D/gaz_2024.txt')
print('towns 2020: %d   2024: %d   joined on ANSICODE: %d' % (len(old), len(new), len(set(old)&set(new))))

# old county FIPS -> planning region FIPS, weighted by town land area
pair=collections.defaultdict(int)
for a in set(old)&set(new):
    oc,_,land = old[a]; nc,_,_ = new[a]
    pair[(oc,nc)] += land
by_old=collections.defaultdict(list)
for (oc,nc),land in pair.items(): by_old[oc].append((land,nc))
print()
print('%-7s %-28s %s' % ('OLDFIPS','DOMINANT PLANNING REGION','share'))
cross={}
for oc in sorted(by_old):
    parts=sorted(by_old[oc], reverse=True); tot=sum(l for l,_ in parts)
    cross[oc]=parts[0][1]
    print('%-7s %-28s %5.1f%%   %s' % (oc, parts[0][1], parts[0][0]/tot*100,
          ('also '+', '.join('%s %.0f%%'%(n,l/tot*100) for l,n in parts[1:])) if len(parts)>1 else ''))
print()
print('crosswalk:', cross)
"
```

### 🤖 Claude · 17:07:40

Done and verified. Reload the tab — Connecticut is on the map.

## Connecticut

Derived the crosswalk from Census rather than memory: downloaded the **2020 and 2024 Gazetteer county-subdivision files for CT** and joined all **169 towns on their stable ANSI codes** (169/169 matched), weighted by land area.

| Old county | Drawn from | Share |
|---|---|---|
| Middlesex | Lower Connecticut River Valley | 100% |
| Windham | Northeastern Connecticut | 94.8% |
| Tolland | Capitol | 93.0% |
| Hartford | Capitol | 87.9% |
| New London | Southeastern Connecticut | 85.9% |
| Litchfield | Northwest Hills | 78.6% |
| Fairfield | Western Connecticut | 72.7% |
| New Haven | South Central Connecticut | 60.8% |

Hartford now reads **$64.70/hr** for Software Developers — exactly the Capitol Planning Region figure.

## AK and NM

Four of the six Alaska cases were just a `City and Borough` suffix (Juneau, Sitka, Wrangell, Yakutat) — handled by extending the strip. Chugach and Copper River both alias onto Valdez-Cordova, the pre-2019 shape they were split from. New Mexico was pure accent, fixed by folding diacritics across the whole lookup, so `Dona Ana` ↔ `Doña Ana` now matches.

**3,122 → 3,136 counties.** Exactly the 14 fixable ones: CT 8, AK 5 (six names, two sharing a shape), NM 1.

## A bug I found while in there

The tooltip was building its key as `info.name + ' County|' + info.stAb`. That only works where the map's name and OFLC's agree — so **every Louisiana parish and Alaska borough showed "No data for this occupation"**, regardless of any of this. Orleans Parish holds a full set of wages and displayed nothing.

It now reads the row `updateMap` already matched to that exact shape, which fixes parishes, boroughs and Connecticut in one go. Verified by hovering: Orleans Parish, LA now shows Level 1 `$33.35` through Average `$41.65`.

## What's still unfixable

The remaining **131** are Puerto Rico (78), Guam (50) and the Virgin Islands (3). `counties-albers-10m` contains only the 50 states plus DC — there is no geometry to colour. That needs a different base map, which is the route-2 call you deferred.

Also unchanged: Naugatuck Valley (Waterbury-Shelton, `$64.23`) doesn't dominate any old county, so that one wage area has no home on a 2020 map. It's the single cost of the approximation, and route 2 would resolve it.

```diff
-   document.addEventListener('click', e => {
    if (!e.target.closest('.autocomplete-wrap')) {
      acList.classList.remove('open');
      locList.classList.remove('open');
    }
  });
+   document.addEventListener('click', e => {
    if (!e.target.closest('.autocomplete-wrap')) {
      acList.classList.remove('open');
      locList.classList.remove('open');
    }
  });

  // ---- CLEAR BUTTONS ----
  // mousedown is suppressed so the field keeps focus; otherwise the blur fires
  // before the click and the caret lands somewhere else.
  document.querySelectorAll('.input-clear').forEach(btn =>
    btn.addEventListener('mousedown', e => e.preventDefault()));

  document.getElementById('filter-clear').addEventListener('click', () => {
    // Just the text, ready for a new search -- the map keeps what it is showing.
    input.value = '';
    acList.classList.remove('open');
    input.focus();
  });

  document.getElementById('location-clear').addEventListener('click', () => {
    // Here the field is the selection, so clear the whole thing: the pin on
    // the map, the county card and the url parameter all go with it.
    locList.classList.remove('open');
    clearCounty();
    locInput.focus();
  });
```

### 🤖 Claude · 17:26:51

Done. Both search boxes have a `×` at the right edge, inset 4px and vertically centred.

```
FILTER BY OCCUPATION                FIND YOUR COUNTY
┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐
│ Software Developers        × │    │ 19104                  × │
└──────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────┘
```

Visibility is CSS, not a JS flag:

```css
.autocomplete-wrap input:not(:placeholder-shown) ~ .input-clear { display: block; }
```

That matters because the value gets set in code as often as it gets typed — selecting an occupation, opening a shared link, loading `?zip=`. A JS flag would need syncing at five call sites and would silently drift; `:placeholder-shown` can't. Confirmed: both × buttons appear on a shared link with no typing at all.

The two do different things, deliberately:

- **Occupation ×** — clears the text only and keeps focus, so you can type a new search. The map keeps what it's showing (3,136 counties, still Software Developers). Wiping the map because you want to retype would be destructive.
- **Location ×** — the field *is* the selection, so it clears the lot: field, the white outline on the map, the county card, and the `zip`/`fips` URL parameter. Same as the existing `clear` link on the card.

`mousedown` is suppressed on both so the field doesn't blur before the click lands.

### 🤖 Claude · 17:30:32

**One folder: `2027Live/`** — 4 files, ~2.6 MB.

```
2027Live/
├── index.html          66 KB   the page
├── disclaimer.txt      311 B   fetched at load; without it a fallback text shows
├── favicon.png        126 KB   <link rel="icon">
└── headerImage.png    2.4 MB   og:image, for link previews
```

That's everything. **No data folders go to the web host** — all 3.2M wage rows are pulled from GitHub at runtime.

## What must stay on GitHub (`Mobile-Version`)

Already pushed by your watcher — listed so you know what not to delete:

| Path | Size | |
|---|---|---|
| `2027/DividedCSV/*.csv.gz` | 35 MB, 848 files | wage data, one per SOC |
| `2027/Prof_w_linksRearrangeColumn.csv` | 271 KB | occupation index + units |
| `2027/zip_county.csv.gz` | 106 KB | ZIP → county |
| `2027/fallback_2025_26.csv.gz` | 14 KB | 2025-26 carry-over |

Served via `media.githubusercontent.com/.../refs/heads/Mobile-Version/`. **If you ever merge to `main` you must update those four URLs in `index.html`**, or point them at `main` now and merge.

Build-only, never uploaded anywhere: `2027/build_2027.py`, `2027/census/`, `OFLC_Wages_2026-27/`, `2026/Data/`.

## One thing to fix before you upload

`index.html:13` references **`apple-touch-icon.png`, which doesn't exist** in the folder — a 404 on every iOS device that saves the page. It's been there since the 2026 page was cloned.

Two options: drop the line, or I generate a 180×180 PNG from `favicon.png`. Say which and I'll do it.

Also worth knowing: `headerImage.png` is **2.4 MB and is 92% of your upload**, used only as the `og:image` for social previews. Resizing it to ~1200×630 would cut it to roughly 100 KB with no visible difference in a link card. Your call — it doesn't affect page load, only the preview fetch.

````
# US County Wage Explorer

**What does your job pay, in your county?** 997 occupations across 3,275 US counties, mapped.

Built on the Department of Labor's prevailing wage data for wage year 2026–27 — the figures
that actually govern H-1B, PERM and other labor certifications. The official tool makes you
pick a state, then a county, then an occupation, then read a table. This one is a map.

**642 MB of government CSVs. A 64 KB web page. No backend, no build step, no server.**

---

## What it does

- **Search 997 occupations** — O*NET titles, not SOC jargon
- **Find your county by ZIP** — type `19104`, get Philadelphia; type `brooklyn`'s county and get Kings
- **All four prevailing wage levels** — entry through senior, plus the average
- **Hourly ⇄ yearly toggle** — converts at 2,080 h/yr, and says so when a figure is converted
- **Rank and career arc** — where your county sits out of 3,136, and how far Level 1 climbs to Level 4
- **Shareable URLs** — `?occ=Software+Developers&zip=19104` restores the exact view

## How it works

```
OFLC_Wages_2026-27/          build_2027.py            2027Live/index.html
  ALC_Export.csv       ──▶   merge, split,      ──▶   fetch one .csv.gz
  Geography.csv              gzip per SOC             per occupation
  xwalk_plus.csv
```

One HTML file. D3 for the map, TopoJSON for the county geometry, everything else hand-rolled.
The wage data lives in Git LFS and is served straight off `media.githubusercontent.com`, so
there is nothing to deploy b
… [truncated, 3792 more chars]
````

```
> The Department of Labor publishes the prevailing wage for every occupation in every US
> county. It's the data behind every H-1B and PERM filing. It ships as 642 MB of CSVs.
>
> I turned it into a map.
>
> Type your ZIP. Type your job. See what it pays where you live, where it pays most, and how
> far the gap goes from entry level to senior.
>
> A few things I learned building it:
>
> → The median occupation pays **2.74× more** in its best county than its worst. For 299 of
> the 847 occupations, the gap is over 3×.
>
> → Connecticut abolished its counties in 2022. Nine planning regions replaced eight
> counties, and most map data hasn't caught up — the whole state renders blank in a lot of
> tools. Fixing it meant joining all 169 Connecticut towns across two Census vintages.
>
> → 63 occupations are published as annual salaries and the rest as hourly rates, with
> nothing in the data to flag which. $236,770 and $87.04 look identical with a dollar sign
> in front of them.
>
> The whole thing is one HTML file. No backend, no build step, no server. 642 MB compresses
> to 34 MB, and a single lookup pulls 39 KB.
>
> LINK
```

## The angle I went with

> **642 MB of government wage data. A 64 KB web page. No backend.**

That's the hook across all of it. It's specific, it's true, and it's the thing another builder reacts to. The human version — *what does your job pay where you live* — leads the README.

## Every number is verified, not estimated

I checked each one against the actual files before writing it:

- **3,265,175** rows · **997** occupations · **848** SOC codes · **3,275** counties
- **642 MB → 33.7 MB** (5.3%), median occupation download **39 KB**
- Page is **64 KB**
- Median occupation pays **2.74×** more in its best county than worst; **299 of 847** exceed 3×
- Athletes **20.85×** · Level 1→4 median **1.60×**, top **9.32×**

I'd cited "Prosthodontists pay the same everywhere" from a SOC code I'd never resolved to a title, so I checked: **3,275 rows, every one `$129.15`, exactly one distinct value.** The claim is literally true.

## The README leads with the hard parts

Not a feature list — the three problems that were genuinely interesting: the hourly/annual ambiguity, Connecticut abolishing its counties, and the ZIP/county-line mess with the measured "it only matters for 6.6% of ZIPs." It also states the limits plainly (territories not drawn, Naugatuck Valley has no shape, informational only).

## Two things before you post
