### 🤖 Claude · 18:36:39

Three things I checked that shape my answer:

- **`AreaName` is parsed and thrown away.** The page shows a county. The official determination is issued for an *area* — "San Jose-Sunnyvale-Santa Clara, CA."
- **Nothing explains Level 1–4** anywhere in the UI.
- **The only proactive popup fires at 30s and asks the user to visit your portfolio.**

## The reframe

I'm assuming your users are mostly H-1B/PERM beneficiaries, the attorneys and HR staff filing for them, and people weighing an offer or a move. If that's wrong, most of this changes.

If it's right, they don't arrive wanting a map. They arrive with a number in their hand asking **"is this okay?"** Right now you give them a beautiful browsing tool and leave them to do the comparison themselves.

## 1. Answer the question they came with

Add a field: **your salary**. Plus occupation and ZIP, which you already have.

```
$120,000 · Software Developers · 95014

Santa Clara, CA — below Level 1
  Level 1  $152,797    ← your offer is $32,797 under
  Level 2  $187,075
  Level 3  $221,374
  Level 4  $255,653
```

That is a consequential answer, and no one gets it from a shaded map. The data is already loaded; it's a comparison and a conversion you're doing anyway. **This is the product** — everything else is browsing around it.

## 2. Say what the levels mean

Zero explanation exists today. For this audience, picking the wrong level is the difference between a clean filing and a rejected one — it keys off experience, education, supervision and judgment. One `?` popover with DOL's actual criteria. It's the cheapest high-value thing on this list.

## 3. Show where the number came from

Every row carries `AreaName` and you discard it. Someone cross-checking against a real LCA needs to see **"San Jose-Sunnyvale-Santa Clara, CA (Area 41940)"**, because that's what the determination says — not "Santa Clara County."

It also quietly explains something users currently find confusing: why Fulton, Forsyth and Cherokee all read exactly `$65.32`. They're one area. Right now that looks like a bug.

## 4. State the wage year and when it expires

OFLC wage years run **July 1 → June 30**. Your title says 2026–27; nothing says *effective 1 Jul 2026 – 30 Jun 2027*. Someone checking in June needs to know whether they're on the right year. For a compliance decision, that's not a detail.

## 5. Two self-inflicted ones

**The 30-second popup.** It's the only moment the product speaks first, and it asks the user for a favour — likely mid-task, right as they're reading wages. Fire it *after* they've gotten their answer, or on exit. Same ask, earned instead of extracted.

**The disclaimer blocks every single visit.** No `localStorage` anywhere. Remember the dismissal — returning users are your most valuable ones and you tax them hardest.

## 6. The quieter ones

- **Mobile** — the branch is literally `Mobile-Version`; this audience checks offers on a phone
- **Accessibility** — no ARIA combobox roles on either search box, and the map has no keyboard path
- **Export** — an attorney will want to save or print a snapshot of a determination

---

**Do 1, 2 and 3.** Together they convert the tool from "look at wage data" into "tell me where I stand, and prove it." 2 and 3 are small. 1 is the one that matters.

Want me to build the offer check?

### 🤖 Claude · 18:40:43

Let me verify the level structure empirically before I write any definitions.

**🔧 Bash**
*Verify the mathematical structure of wage levels*
```bash
cd /Users/shubhamarundekatey/OFLCDashboard && ~/anaconda3/bin/python3 -I -W ignore -c "
import gzip, csv, random, statistics
rows=[]
for soc in ('15-1252','29-1141','13-2011','17-2051','25-2021'):
    for r in csv.DictReader(gzip.open('2027/DividedCSV/%s.csv.gz'%soc,'rt')):
        try: rows.append((soc, float(r['Level1']),float(r['Level2']),float(r['Level3']),float(r['Level4']),float(r['Average'])))
        except ValueError: pass
print('sampled rows:', len(rows))
# Are L2/L3 evenly spaced between L1 and L4?
lin=0; off=[]
for s,l1,l2,l3,l4,av in rows:
    step=(l4-l1)/3
    e2,e3=l1+step, l1+2*step
    if abs(l2-e2)<0.02 and abs(l3-e3)<0.02: lin+=1
    else: off.append((s,l1,l2,l3,l4,round(e2,2),round(e3,2)))
print('L2,L3 exactly linear between L1 and L4: %d/%d (%.2f%%)' % (lin,len(rows),lin/len(rows)*100))
if off: print('  exceptions, first 3:', off[:3])
print()
# where does Average sit relative to the levels?
pos=[(av-l1)/(l4-l1) for s,l1,l2,l3,l4,av in rows if l4>l1]
print('Average sits at %.3f of the way from L1 to L4 (median), min %.3f max %.3f' % (statistics.median(pos),min(pos),max(pos)))
print('  i.e. Average is %s' % ('≈ Level 3' if abs(statistics.median(pos)-2/3)<0.05 else 'elsewhere'))
print()
d=[(l2-l1,l3-l2,l4-l3) for s,l1,l2,l3,l4,av in rows]
print('sample row gaps (L1→L2, L2→L3, L3→L4):', [tuple(round(x,2) for x in g) for g in d[:3]])
"
```

import gzip, csv, glob, random

n=hit=0; worst=0

print('largest deviation: %.4f' % worst)
"

### 🤖 Claude · 18:46:10

Done. A `?` sits next to **METRIC** in the header; it opens an explainer and closes on the button, the backdrop, or Escape.

## The content is sourced, not recalled

I wouldn't write compliance-adjacent definitions from memory. A subagent found the official DOL guidance but couldn't extract the PDF text, so I installed `pypdf` and pulled it myself — **Prevailing Wage Determination Policy Guidance, revised November 2009**. All four definitions are condensed from the actual document:

| | |
|---|---|
| **Level 1 — Entry** | Basic understanding; routine tasks, limited exercise of judgment; close supervision, work monitored for accuracy |
| **Level 2 — Qualified** | Good understanding via education or experience; moderately complex tasks, limited judgment |
| **Level 3 — Experienced** | Sound understanding plus special skills; exercises judgment, may coordinate or supervise staff |
| **Level 4 — Fully competent** | Plans and conducts work independently; advanced skills on unusual and complex problems; technical guidance only; generally supervisory |

I kept DOL's own *indicators* too, since they're the practical part — "research fellow, worker in training, or an internship" points at Level 1; "lead, senior, head, chief, journeyman" points at Level 3.

## Two structural facts, measured not quoted

While checking the levels I found something that changes how to read your own UI:

**Levels 2 and 3 aren't measured — they're interpolated.** Verified across 36,025 rows: Level 2 and Level 3 sit exactly evenly spaced between Level 1 and Level 4. Santa Clara software developers: `$73.46 → $89.94 → $106.43 → $122.91`, gaps of `16.48, 16.49, 16.48`.

That means **the career arc you asked me to build is always a straight line.** It shows slope, never shape — not a rendering artifact, just what the data is. The explainer says so, so nobody reads meaning into its flatness.

**"Average" is a fifth figure, not a mean of the four.** It lands consistently at ~67% of the Level 1→Level 4 span (measured range 0.669–0.671 across 36,025 rows) — just above Level 3.

Both are labelled in the modal as *measured from this dataset, not quoted from DOL*, because the 2009 guidance states neither.

## What I deliberately left out

I'd have liked to include the percentile basis (Level 1 = 17th, Level 4 = 67th). **The guidance doesn't state it**, and `grep` for "percentile" in the full 36 pages returned nothing. I know the figures from general knowledge, but on a page people may use for a visa filing, unverified is unverified — so it isn't there.

The modal also carries a one-line disclaimer that it isn't advice on which level applies to your job, and links the source PDF.

     Unit handling (module scope, block 1):
     ```js
     let activeUnit = 'H';
     let userUnitPref = null;              // null = as published
     const HOURS_PER_YEAR = 2080;
     function displayUnit() { return userUnitPref || activeUnit; }
     function isConverted() { return displayUnit() !== activeUnit; }
     function toDisplay(v) {
       if (!isConverted()) return v;
       return displayUnit() === 'A' ? v * HOURS_PER_YEAR : v / HOURS_PER_YEAR;
     }
     function fmtNum(v) {
       if (!v) return 'N/A';
       const x = toDisplay(v);
       return displayUnit() === 'A'
         ? '$' + Math.round(x).toLocaleString('en-US')
         : '$' + x.toFixed(2);
     }
     function fmt(v) { return v ? fmtNum(v) + unitSuffix() : 'N/A'; }
     function unitSuffix() { return displayUnit() === 'A' ? '/yr' : '/hr'; }
     function unitWords() {
       return (displayUnit() === 'A' ? 'per year' : 'per hour') +
              (isConverted() ? ' (converted)' : '');
     }
     ```

     Fallback overlay loader:

     const FALLBACK_URL = 'https://media.githubusercontent.com/media/deka1105/OFLCDashboard/refs/heads/Mobile-Version/2027/fallback_2025_26.csv.gz';

     const fold = s => s.normalize('NFD').replace(/[\u0300-\u036f]/g, '');
     const COUNTY_ALIASES = {
       'Capitol Planning Region|CT': ['09003', '09013'],
       'Lower Connecticut River Valley Planning Region|CT': ['09007'],
       'Northeastern Connecticut Planning Region|CT': ['09015'],
       'Northwest Hills Planning Region|CT': ['09005'],
       'South Central Connecticut Planning Region|CT': ['09009'],
       'Southeastern Connecticut Planning Region|CT': ['09011'],
       'Western Connecticut Planning Region|CT': ['09001'],
       'Chugach Census Area|AK': ['02261'],
       'Copper River Census Area|AK': ['02261']
     };
     function resolveCountyFipsList(county, stAb) {
       const alias = COUNTY_ALIASES[county + '|' + stAb];
       if (alias) return alias;
       const tries = [county];
       const stripped = county
         .replace(/ City and Borough$| County$| Parish$| Borough$| Census Area$| Municipality$| Municipio$| city$| City$/, '')
         .trim();
       tries.push(stripped, stripped + ' County', stripped + ' Parish');
       for (const t of tries) {
         const hit = nameFipsMap[t + '|' + stAb] || nameFipsMap[fold(t) + '|' + stAb];
         if (hit) return [hit];
       }
       return [];
     }
